The low-degree cases.
1. Suppose j = 2 and D = 2. The two edges join the same blocks, so use the repeated-edge
   calculation following (E5). Its coefficient combines with the square counterterm because
   both multiply the same projected pair square. After extracting the common skeleton
   measure and c2 /4, their coefficient before differentiation is
                               n                                            o
                             b2 Elow PA PB − c2 (Elow MA MB )2 − b.

   The constant term b2 χ2 − b, and its unscaled derivatives, are O(λc2 ) because b◦ χ2 = 1
   and b − b◦ = O(λc2 ). The next two terms from (E8), after replacing b by b◦ , are

                                   b2◦ χ3 k0 c2 ,         1 2 2 2 2
                                                          2 b◦ g k0 vAB .

   Their direct derivatives are O(λc2 ); their size contributions cancel by the j = 2 row
   of the table. Substitution errors from b − b◦ have the required bound. The remaining
   O∗ (c3 ) errors, including the smaller product-of-means term, contribute O(c4 ) after the
   net compression factor c. On size paths this is O(λc3 ) because c ≲ λ; on direct paths the
   derivative supplies λ itself. Thus (E4) holds. A defect factor supplies λc2 directly and
   gives the same bound, with the allowed O⋄ (1) loss.
2. Suppose j = 2 and D = 3, with edges AB, AC. For the one-part partition, (E9) gives
   the leading coefficient b2◦ χ3 k0 vBC , with net compression factor c2 . Its size contribution
   cancels by the same row of the table; its direct derivative costs O(λc3 ), or O(λc2 h′ )
   when vBC = h′ is the earlier split. The error is O∗ (c2 ), or O∗ (ch′ ) when the designated
   vertices are distinct and their earlier mass is h′ . Multiplication by c2 , followed on size
   paths by c ≲ λ, gives respectively O(λc3 ) and O(λc2 h′ ). The errors from b − b◦ and any
   defect factors satisfy these same bounds. For the two-part partition the product of pair
   moments is O∗ (vAB vAC ), with net factor c3 , which is smaller still. Since h′ ≲ mins rs ,
   the refined bound gives the extra ratio required in (E4).
3. Suppose j = 2 and D = 4. Both partitions have net factor c3 , and their products of
   moments are O∗ (c2 ). When the designated vertices are distinct with earlier mass h′ ,
   these products are also O∗ (h′ ). For a product of two pair moments this follows at once
   from their individual bounds. For the four-spin moment, condition on the prefixes at h′ .
   The earlier edge has separated ends, while the ends of the later edge are still together.
   Hence at least two of the independent future groups have odd parity. Each such group’s
   conditional expectation vanishes at zero starting field and has a polynomially bounded
   spatial derivative,
             √         also after parameter differentiation. The common prefix fields have
   size O∗ ( h ), so the product of these two odd expectations gives O∗ (h′ ).
                ′

   On size paths the refined contribution is therefore O(c3 h′ ) ≤ O(λc2 h′ ), exactly the
   required smaller-scale bound. On direct paths the derivative supplies λ. The unrefined
   O∗ (c2 ) estimate gives (E4) without the ratio. Extracting defect factors preserves both
   conclusions.
4. Suppose j = 3, for which the target is O⋄ (λc2 ). Terms with defects or with Dπ ≥ 4
   already have enough powers of c: on a size path one extra power is bounded by λ, and on
   a direct path the derivative supplies λ. It remains to consider one-part partitions with two
   or three blocks. For D = 2, each block has degree three and (E7) gives g 2 k0 vAB + O∗ (c2 ).
   For D = 3, an odd block degree gives O∗ (c); otherwise all three degrees are two, giving
   χ3 + O∗ (c). The two leading monomials after multiplication by b3◦ are precisely those in
   the j = 3 row of the table, so their size contributions cancel. With net factors c and c2 ,
   respectively, the remaining errors are O(c3 ), which is at most O(λc2 ). Direct derivatives
   give the same target without this cancellation.

                                                    115
5. Suppose j = 4, for which the target is O⋄ (λc). Only size terms with no defect and Dπ = 2
   lack the required power before cancellation. Such a term has one part, on two blocks of
   degree four each. Its moment is g 2 + O∗ (c). Multiplication by b4◦ gives the last row of the
   table, so the leading size contribution cancels. The remainder with net factor c is O(c2 ),
   at most O(λc). All other terms have an additional compression power, a defect factor, or
   the direct derivative factor λ.
The exponentially small terminal errors retain the mass factors of their corresponding main
terms. Since M = log3 (1/x), they are smaller than every fixed power of x. This proves
(E4).

    The row expansion, the high-mass estimates, and the low-degree coefficient bound now
give both increments required in Proposition 4.1. The final parameter choice in Section 10
makes their savings simultaneous and justifies the stated uniformity for moving site weights.


10     Parameter choices and completion of the proof
Recall that x = N −1/6 and x12 ≤ λ ≤ xp∗ . The parameters below are fixed independently of
N . We first choose the savings used to control size changes, then the final-strength exponent
p∗ , and only then the truncation and loss parameters for direct changes. The essential order
is that the size Taylor degrees determine the polynomial loss constants before the cutoffs
and p∗ are selected. Direct Taylor degrees may be larger, because their estimates do not
enter that size choice. Every degree, moment order, and loss exponent below is fixed before
N → ∞.
1. Fix T > 1 and the neighborhoods, tapers, and constants c1 , cb in (P1)–(P7), leaving
   strict room in the admissibility and derivative bounds. The global saving cG in (G1) is
   available before choosing s∗ near 1/2 or the ultimate small p∗ . In its proof, the coarse
   clock estimate (G3) supplies a fixed saving first. Choose ag below that saving, then dg
   small enough to absorb the fixed inverse-kernel loss in the covariance estimate following
   (G4), and finally the PE power slack for this global comparison small relative to dg . The
   endpoint condition (P9) uses the common base temperature as explained in Section 6. It
   requires λ → 0, without requiring λ ≤ xag . Thus later decreases of p∗ do not decrease cG .
2. Set kc = 0.03. Choose τ ≪ min(10−4 , cG ), put s∗ = 1/2 − τ , and then choose η ≪ τ and
   τ0 ≪ η, with s0 = s∗ − τ0 , as in (C1)–(C2). Choose bs > 0 so that

                    2(τ + τ0 ) + bs < min(cG /12, kc /3),        bs < 2(η − τ0 ),

   and then bℓ ≪ bs . These inequalities leave room for the local comparison errors throughout
   λ ≥ x12 . Choose also he ≪ min(1, cG ).
   Both final-strength estimates in (C2) now have a fixed saving c > 0 for every sufficiently
   small p∗ . Indeed, with λ = xp∗ , U = xλ−s∗ /2 , and the edge seed off, the differentiated

                       4 + c G − p∗ ,    5 + p∗ (2η − 5s∗ /2),       6 − 2p∗ .

   for example (1/200)/50, under the preliminary restriction p∗ < (1/200)/100. Neither
   saving depends on the remaining choice of p∗ .

   margins in Section 7; in particular, it is smaller than p∗ times each required gap in a
   λ-exponent, including the gaps involving bℓ and τ0 . Then choose the local PE power slacks
   small relative to ζ, and the buffer exponent ρ small enough for (C18). The remaining
   finite profile and moment iterations, and their subpower losses, can be chosen with these
   parameters fixed.
   The extra optimized excess O(x4−2ζ uΛ1/3 ) fits the budget at (C3): after division by
   λu, its ratio to x4 λ−2s0 is x−2ζ λkc /3−1+2s0 , which is power small by these choices. The
                                          ′
   final-strength switch error O(x5+c ) from (P11) also fits after division by λu. At the
                                                           ′
   smallest dyad u ≍ U , its ratio to U 4 is at most xc λ5s∗ /2−1 ; larger dyads improve this
   bound. Both exponents are positive, so the switch leaves room for the remaining losses.
5. For the direct paths, choose the coarse cutoff exponent κ > 0 small relative to the gains
   in Lemma 8.8. More precisely, its large-Y event costs x−4κ , so choose

                             4κ < min {p∗ (1 − 2s∗ ), (1 − η)/s∗ − 2} .

   Both quantities on the right are positive by the choices of s∗ , η. The gains used in the
   remaining direct expansion are

            1 − 2s∗ = 2τ > 0,       (1 − η)/2 − s∗ = τ − η/2 > 0,          s∗ /2 − η > 0.

   A factor λg with any of these exponents g > 0 is at most xp∗ g . Choose δ ′ > 0 smaller

                                    C0 δ ′ < p∗ (1 − η)/2 − s∗ ,

   with strict room for the later losses. Thus the direct Taylor error x5−C0 δ λ(1−η)/2−s∗
   saves a power over x5 . Now fix the direct pressure Taylor degree large enough also for
   this δ ′ . The initial direct projection cost satisfies x6 λ−η ≤ x6−12η , so its margin over x5
   is 1 − 12η > 0.

                                                117
   Choose the fixed moment and change-of-measure orders large enough that truncation
   and transfer lose only small fractions of the margins just retained. Then choose the
   geometric loss in (D1) and (D10), and the subpower losses, still smaller. The smooth
   density comparisons on intervals of length O(bϵ ) may require higher fixed orders after this
   geometric choice; they change constants only. Finally take the stencil order large enough
   for both the fixed size pressure polynomial and the site-weight smoothing in Section 4.
   Enlarging this order does not require enlarging the size Taylor degree.
    These choices give a common positive saving in the stopped increment bounds. For a
direct strength sweep, Lemma 8.8 handles Y > xκ ; on the complement apply the allocation
and compression estimates (E3), (E4) to the pressure polynomial (E1). The two multiplier
sweeps use the separate switch bounds of the same lemma. The size sweep uses the capped
low statistics, (D15), and the size versions of these polynomial estimates. Integrating the
direct strength parameter costs only O(log(1/x)). The size segment has bounded length,
                                                                       −1/2
and the multiplier bound has at most the integrable endpoint factor χe . The fixed-degree
dyadic and projection-cut sums cost subpower factors. Decreasing the common saving
absorbs these integration and summation costs.
    We spell out the uniformity needed when the site count varies. At each fixed size-path
point θ, every estimate above is applied to the particular integer m being tested, uniformly
for N/2 ≤ m ≤ 3N . In particular, the averaging in (C1)–(C2) is over γ at this fixed (θ, m).
The physical clocks are common to these site counts. Their absolute dotted-clock norms
are bounded up to subpower loss as explained after (P8); integrating any remaining dotted
weight in mass therefore gives a common integrable envelope in θ. Thus the bound is uniform
in m before integrating θ.
    Let wm (θ) ≥ 0 be measurable probability weights on the allowed integers. Multiplying
the parameter-averaged bound at (θ, m) by wm (θ) and summing preserves its common
                  P
envelope, since m wm (θ) = 1. A measurable choice of a single m(θ) is the special case
of a point mass. Fixed-size path approximation makes the pressure tests and statistics
measurable, and absolute continuity supplies their a.e. path derivatives. Fubini first removes
a null set of θ for each parameter-averaged estimate. At fixed N there are only finitely many
allowed m, so their exceptional sets can then be united into one null set before weighting
and integrating.
    For each summand the change of measure is used only on the central stop Km (s) ≤ 3xε .
The comparisons with finitely many added rows in (A6)–(A9) have bounded covariance
changes and estimate their remainders with moments of the original m-site system. They
therefore require this same stop, without a new stopping condition for the added-row
systems. Fix the common saving before taking the small Laplace exponent ε in Section 4;
this exponent is distinct from the geometric slack. The density bounds on the stop hold for
every fixed finite order, so they cover all transfer orders chosen above, for every sufficiently
small fixed ε > 0 and each fixed test coefficient q > 0 in s = qx1+ε .
    For one such test, the same parameter bounds cover the finitely many segments in the
comparison from N to any N ′ ∈ [N, 2N ]. Summing their stopped bounds and then choosing
γ gives the single route used in Section 4. Different tests may choose different routes: their
bare endpoint laws are independent of γ. Thus the later interpolation at finitely many
Laplace tests requires no parameter choice uniform over an infinite test family.
    This proves Proposition 4.1, including its version with a measurably varying site count or
site weights. Section 4 then proves Theorem 1.2 and proves Proposition 1.3. Removing the
independent Gaussian completion and dividing by the exact finite-size standard deviation,
as done there, proves Theorem 1.1 for every fixed β > 1.

and existing comparison inequalities. We keep the finite-size normalizations explicit.

A     Inputs from the fluctuation-scale companion
We collect the inputs from [19], with their conventions and their uses in this paper. The
moment and comparison estimates are separate inputs from the exponent theorem. The
target temperature is fixed in (1, ∞); the auxiliary common bases Tm → T > 1 are treated
below. All replica tests have a fixed finite number of labels, and all moment, Taylor and
recursion orders are fixed before the system size tends to infinity.

A.1     Covariance and mass normalization
The Hamiltonian in (1.1), multiplied by β, has covariance mT Q2 /2 − T /2. Adding the
independent spin-constant Gaussian of variance T /2 in (1.3) gives mT Q2 /2, exactly the
covariance of [19]. The probability spin prior subtracts m log 2. Consequently the centered
completed variable in the companion is our Fm  c − EF c .

A.2     Finite-tree calculus and its probability laws
The following list specifies both the source statement and the additional conditions retained
when it is used.




                                                   120
1. [19, Lemmas 2.1–2.3, equation (1)] give the source Hessian, pressure variation, clock
   inequality, and single-spin pinned moments in Lemma 2.1. The pinned estimate requires
   zero deterministic external field, deterministic weights, and the full single-path law. If
   other branches are present, their normalized kernels are first marginalized; remaining
   random factors are treated afterwards by unconditional Hölder. The polynomial form
   (A2) is stated and proved in [19, Appendix, “Source and pressure calculus; the scope of
   pinned moments”]. Section 2 recalls its proof in our normalization: for fixed terminal
   spin the gradient of Pv (Lv ) is Pv′ (Lv ) times the original gradient. The same block-Hessian
   inequality and Brascamp–Lieb argument apply, and gauge symmetry removes the pin
   without changing the centering. The coefficients of Pv are deterministic under the positive
   path law. This estimate is used in Lemma 8.6.
2. [19, Lemma 3.5, equation (13)] supplies (A3). The anchor is determined by the prefix
   and branches exterior to the future being projected. Conditional on those data, that
   future must retain its normalized positive kernel. The traversed field length and its
   positive lower mass are bounded below by inverse powers of size. In every application
   here, the total clocks are at most a fixed power of log m, as stipulated in Section 2; hence
   the coefficient ratios in the finite projection iteration are polynomial in the size. The
   lemma itself does not require the optimized constraints of PE. The layer estimates in
   Sections 6–8 use this version with unit-normalized anchors and then integrate the anchor
   magnitude. Their Hessian blocking is the calculation in [19, proof of Proposition 3.4,
   preceding equation (15)].
3. [19, Proposition 2.5, Lemmas 2.6, 2.8 and 2.9, equation (2)] give the real-power allocation
   rule, row sums, bounded split densities, and fixed-order Taylor estimates. Each inde-
   pendent Gaussian increment covariance remains positive semidefinite throughout each
   interpolation. Spin tests and their scalar centers are held fixed during these interpola-
   tions; a varying test requires its own product-rule derivatives. These are the inputs to
   Lemmas 2.2–2.5, the coefficient algebra in Proposition 5.1, and the compression identity
   (E5). A row collapses only after its summand is independent of the new label’s detailed
   attachment. Allocation order is changed before absolute values are taken. A reused fork
   retains its atom; a new fork has a continuous mass variable.
4. [19, Corollary 2.10] bounds fixed-order source derivatives. The terminal-datum derivatives
   in Lemma 3.3 are treated by its diffusion representation with one bounded smooth
   insertion; the source bounds control the remaining fixed derivative slots. The scalar time
   identities are those of [19, Lemma 5.3], converted from time K/T to variance time K.
   They are also used for the single-coordinate freezing estimate in (D11).
5. [19, Proposition 2.14] is a limit at each fixed system size, with fixed finite observed
   topology. Terminal clocks and every prescribed cut or revelation side are retained. No
   rate uniform in system size is inferred. At a simultaneous jump we choose the joint linear
   filling
                             (b− , h− ) + t(∆b, ∆h),     0 ≤ t ≤ 1.
   If an intermediate filled revelation is observed, approximations preserve this filling and
   its specified observations. Integrated clock identities refer to that chosen filling; only
   endpoint laws are independent of it. Nonnegative contributions of filled jumps can be
   discarded in trace upper bounds. Fixed-size limits are used in Lemma 2.1, the scalar
   path construction, Lemma 4.2, and the finite-resolution passage in Proposition 5.1. The
   latter first uses the inverse at a finite resolution with its uniform absolute attachment
   bound; it does not require convergence of the inverse kernels themselves.




                                              121
    For the centered-coupling argument of [19, Lemmas 2.11–2.13 and 4.5], the conditioning
sigma-field is generated by the common prefix, the unaffected exterior branches, and the
fixed topology. It contains no observation of the affected future. This preserves the two
separate conditional marginals. The weighted exterior anchor in [19, Lemma 4.8] averages
the exterior joint law rather than conditioning on its terminal spins. The applications here
retain these conditions. In particular Lemma 8.12 and Remark 8.13 explicitly construct a
marginal law independent of the moving fork before using a centered integral.

A.3    Optimized comparisons and changed constraints
The all-mass calls in Sections 6 and 7 use [19, Definition 6.1 and Proposition 6.6], with data
(3)–(6) there. Writing m for the site count and ϵPE > 0 for its fixed power slack, the error
parameter obeys
                              m−2/3+ϵPE dPE ≲ E ≤ m−1/2 d2PE .
The bare paths and all changed competitors are admissible, total clocks are uniformly
bounded, and a fixed positive matrix margin remains after the comparison. The upper
bound for d2PE has a fixed positive gap below the limiting T . The endpoint condition is
(4) of PE on every required comparable mass band, with the common base Tm of (P9).
The common-base extension is stated and proved in [19, Appendix, “A common prelimit
temperature”]. Section 6 verifies its use here and recalls the substitutions J0 = Tm χ2 − 1 in
PE (26)–(27) and time K/Tm in PE Lemmas 5.4–5.5. This base stays fixed through each
finite parent/child tree. The macroscopic limit is at the fixed T > 1, so [19, Lemma 5.8 and
Proposition 5.9] apply at that limit. No uniform assertion near the critical temperature is
required.
     For clarity, the further source inputs to the modified local optimization are as follows.
1. The geometry of [19, Lemma 3.3, equations (9)–(10)] is rederived for the prescribed
   density box in Lemma 7.2. The first variation has no state-dependent integrating factor
   in this box. The original PE density cap is not assumed for its minimizers.
2. Under the stated outer-regularity hypotheses, Lemmas 7.4 and 7.5 establish the replace-
   ment raw and projection estimates (C8)–(C9) and second moments (C10), including
   the enhanced floor. Under the additional strong-removal hypotheses, Lemma 7.6 gives
   separate mass and radius estimates and one good calibration cut for all fixed moment
   orders. For cap/contact units these proofs use [19, Lemmas 3.11–3.12, equations (20)–
   (21)]: whole-cap trace bounds, controlled mean oscillation, deterministic weights, and
   comparable masses. Broad caps are sliced with their additional trace cost retained. These
   same inputs are used in Lemmas 7.11, 7.12 and 7.13. The enhanced floor is treated
   separately by radius slides; small physical floor mass is never used to discard radius
   intervals.
3. The weak-removal argument uses the child-data proof of [19, Lemma 6.2], the movement
   inequality of [19, Lemma 6.3], and feasible-barrier arguments of [19, Lemma 6.4]. The
   child has the ordinary PE constraints. The parent movements are proved feasible for
   the new density box before using movement. Child success gives agreement and then
   outer regularity for the child, as in [19, Lemma 6.5]; only then are [19, Theorem 5.1 and
   Proposition 5.9] used.
4. Proposition 7.7, used in the strong-removal argument, reuses the proof of [19, Proposi-
   tion 4.1], rather than applying its original optimization hypotheses to the new density
   box. The preceding analytic estimates replace PE (12)–(13), (16)–(19). Fixed-degree
   tests remain those of [19, Definition 4.2]; bounded row changes, observed-block trun-
   cation, boundary covariance, test-family closure, and projected replacements are [19,

                                             122
    Lemmas 4.3–4.8]. The finite-depth cancellation and calibration are [19, Lemmas 4.9–4.10,
    equations (23)–(27)]. The division is by χK∗ ≍ W , not by a stability gap. One common
    good cut is used for the fixed finite recursion and its required moment orders; these orders
    are fixed before the size limit. The resulting moving-target estimate is stated explicitly
    in (7.1); (7.2) gives its two integrated forms, with the mass and radius normalizations
    kept separate. These are the versions of PE (22) used here. The profile proof is then
    repeated using [19, Lemmas 5.3–5.6] and PE (28)–(29). Identification of its slope uses
    [19, Lemma 5.8 and Proposition 5.9] and the macroscopic transfer in Proposition 7.3.
    The coarse scalar trial used for (G3) comes from the bounds in the proof of [19,
Lemma 5.7], with the explicit floor, cap, and cutoff choices given in Section 6. Its proof is
used quantitatively; the limiting variational statement alone would not provide the needed
power saving. The scalar control representation used for coercivity and pair-kernel positivity
is proved in [19, proof of Lemma 5.8] by completing the square in Itô’s formula.

A.4        The exponent and the one-dimensional input
The fixed-temperature exponent theorem [19, Theorem 1.1] is used twice in Section 4:
to initialize the stopped small-positive-Laplace comparison, and to rule out zero limiting
variance after a power rate of variance convergence has been obtained. The first use combines
the exponent with [19, Lemmas 7.6–7.7]; along the nonbare physical paths the appropriate
exponential augmentation is instead proved in Lemma 4.2 from the telescoped pinned density.
That proof uses the full joint positive law and then gauge symmetry. The one-dimensional
tail, positive-tail-mass and small-Laplace bounds are exactly [19, Lemma 7.7].
    The exponent supplies neither a variance prefactor nor a limiting law. Those conclusions
use the summable increment estimate of this paper. Finally Var Fm    c = Var F + T /2; the
                                                                                m
common Gaussian therefore disappears after division by m1/6 . The theorem is stated for
log partition functions. For the physical total free energy −Fm /β, the variance constant is
divided by β 2 and the standardized limiting law is reflected.
